# aster-code-review

Review code against Asterinas's coding guidelines
— the persona-keyed pages under `book/src/to-contribute/coding-guidelines/`
— and write one Markdown review file.
The review is **recall-first**: miss as few real defects as possible.
It makes both objective calls (undeniable bugs) and subjective calls (guideline violations, each grounded in a cited rule).

There are two review modes, both **anchored at the current checkout (HEAD)**:

- **`diff <base>`** — a Git change:
  the **commit series** the branch adds over its merge-base with `<base>`
  — each commit's message *and* its diff,
  so the review covers per-commit intent and commit hygiene, not just the net change.
  (Commit first; uncommitted edits are not reviewed in diff mode.)
- **`files <path[:lines] ...>`**
  — target code: the working-tree contents of the named files,
  optionally narrowed to line ranges.

To review at a specific commit, check it out first (it becomes HEAD).

This skill is agent-agnostic (Claude Code and Codex).
Only step 3's spawn primitive differs between agents;
everything else is identical.

## Interface

However the skill is triggered,
it receives ONE raw argument string that begins with a mode word:

```
diff   <base>              <output> [--overwrite] [--per-persona-context=auto|yes|no]
files  <path[:lines] ...>  <output> [--overwrite] [--per-persona-context=auto|yes|no]
```

- `<base>` (diff) — **required**,
  any ref or SHA;
  reviews the commit series `merge-base(<base>, HEAD)..HEAD` (each commit's message + diff).
- `<path[:lines] ...>` (files) — **required**, one or more targets in the working tree.
  A target is a path, optionally `path:N-M` or `path:N-M,K-L` (1-based, inclusive);
  repeat a path to add ranges.
  Wrap a path in double quotes to allow spaces or colons (`"a: b.rs":10-20`).
- `<output>` — **required, last positional** (`cp src... dest` style);
  refuse to overwrite unless `--overwrite`.
- `--overwrite` — replace the output file if it already exists.
- `--per-persona-context` — `yes`: fan out one isolated agent per persona (best recall).
  `no`: one combined agent reviews all personas (cheaper, lower recall).
  `auto` (default) currently resolves to `yes`;
  it will become a benchmark-driven heuristic.
  Controls step 3 only.

**Do not pre-split the argument string yourself.**
Pass it verbatim, as a single quoted argument,
to `resolve_target.sh` (step 1)
— the script self-tokenizes (whitespace-split except inside double quotes),
which keeps the parse deterministic and identical across agents.

## Pipeline

Run these steps in order.
Steps 1 and 5 are deterministic scripts;
steps 6–8 use the model.

The helper scripts live in the skill's own `scripts/` directory,
at `.agents/skills/aster-code-review/scripts/` inside the repository under review.
**Invoke every script by its absolute path**
— the working directory is the code under review (the repo root), *not* the skill directory,
so a bare `scripts/…` is not found (notably on Codex, which runs commands from the repo root; do not `cd` into the skill dir either — `resolve_target.sh` needs the repo as its cwd).
Shells do not persist between commands,
so **set `SKILL` at the start of each command that uses it** (or inline the `$(git rev-parse …)`):

```sh
SKILL="$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)/.agents/skills/aster-code-review"; "$SKILL/scripts/resolve_target.sh" '<raw args>'
```

The steps below write `$SKILL/scripts/…` as shorthand for that absolute path.

1. **Resolve the target.**
   `"$SKILL/scripts/resolve_target.sh" '<raw args>'` prints the canonical review input
   — the commit series (each commit's message + diff) in `diff` mode,
   annotated file excerpts in `files` mode;
   save it to a temp file.
   Run it again with `--meta` first (`"$SKILL/scripts/resolve_target.sh" --meta '<raw args>'`) to get
   `mode=`, `base=`/`files=`, `head=`, `branch=`, `output=`, `overwrite=`, `per_persona_context=`;
   add `date=` (today) and an optional `title=` to make the meta file.
   Pass the raw argument string as a single quoted argument
   so the script's tokenizer sees it intact.
2. **Activate personas.**
   Pick which of the five personas run,
   from the reviewed paths — changed paths in `diff` mode,
   named paths in `files` mode (see *Activation*).
3. **Fan out.**
   Spawn the persona passes (see *Spawning*).
   With `per_persona_context` = `yes` or `auto` (the default),
   run **one isolated PASS per activated persona** — best recall.
   With `no`, run **one combined PASS** over all activated personas in a single context
   — cheaper, lower recall.
   Build each pass's prompt deterministically with `"$SKILL/scripts/build_pass_prompt.sh" <input-file> <persona>...`
   — one persona for fan-out, all activated personas for combined
   — and spawn the sub-agent with **that exact text**.
   Each pass returns a JSON array of comments;
   file each under its persona's `<fragdir>/<persona>.json` (the comment's `persona` field says which),
   so step 5 is unchanged.
4. **Collect** the per-persona JSON fragments.
5. **Assemble.**
   `"$SKILL/scripts/assemble_review.sh" [--overwrite] <meta> <fragdir> <output>` performs the deterministic merge (group by persona in fixed order, sort by file→line, drop exact duplicates *within a persona*, write frontmatter, leave a `<!-- SUMMARY -->` placeholder).
   Pass `--overwrite` only if the user gave it;
   otherwise the script refuses to clobber an existing `<output>`.
6. **Verify** (see *Verification*).
7. **Consolidate** (see *Consolidation*).
8. **Summary.**
   Replace `<!-- SUMMARY -->` with a constructive summary over the final comments:
   what the code does well, the top issues ranked by severity,
   any structural recommendations.

## Activation

A persona runs unless the reviewed paths *provably* contain nothing in its remit (reviewed paths = changed paths in `diff` mode, named paths in `files` mode):

- **maintainability, development, security** — any reviewed code.
- **hardware** — a reviewed path is assembly (`*.S`, `*.asm`),
  an architecture directory (`.../arch/...`), or contains `asm!` / `global_asm!`.
- **documentation** — a reviewed path is `book/`, any `*.md`,
  syscall-coverage files (`*.scml`), or a user-facing API surface (a syscall or kernel parameter).

Activation is path-based and deterministic;
do not use a model to triage (a wrongly-skipped persona is a silent recall hole).

## Pass contract

The shared reviewer contract
— the review methodology and the JSON comment schema every pass must follow
— lives in [`scripts/pass_contract.md`](scripts/pass_contract.md),
inlined as the **stable head** of every pass prompt by `build_pass_prompt.sh` (step 3).
Do not paraphrase it into the prompt yourself:
retyping it breaks the byte-identical prefix the prompt cache relies on (see [`execution_model.md`](spec/execution_model.md)).
A pass reads only the persona block(s) it is given (selective exposure),
reviews the **REVIEW INPUT** at the foot of the prompt,
and returns a JSON array of comments per that schema.

## Spawning a pass

Build every pass prompt with `"$SKILL/scripts/build_pass_prompt.sh" <input-file> <persona>...`
and spawn the sub-agent with its **exact** output
— only a script keeps the prompt's stable head (contract + guideline) byte-identical across passes and reviews,
which is what lets the prompt cache reuse it (see [`execution_model.md`](spec/execution_model.md)).

**Default (`per_persona_context` = `yes`/`auto`)**
— one pass per activated persona,
each in a CLEAN context with only its own persona block (selective exposure):

- **Claude Code** — spawn a Task sub-agent per persona.
- **Codex** — run `codex exec "<build_pass_prompt.sh output>"` per persona
  (pass the built PROMPT TEXT as the argument).

**Never spawn a pass by re-running `aster_code_review.sh` / `run_agent.sh`,
nor by re-issuing the skill's own arguments** (e.g. `codex exec … diff <base> <out>`).
A pass is a *reviewer* invocation whose input is the `build_pass_prompt.sh` text
— not another run of this skill.
Re-entering the launcher spawns another orchestrator that spawns another … , an infinite fork bomb;
the launcher now refuses it (`ACR_AGENT_RUNNING`).

Passes are independent;
order does not matter.
Collect each pass's JSON.

**Combined (`per_persona_context` = `no`)**
— build ONE prompt with all activated personas (`"$SKILL/scripts/build_pass_prompt.sh" <input> <p1> <p2> ...`)
and run a single pass;
it reviews through every lens at once and tags each comment with its `persona`.
The input and spawn overhead are paid once,
so it is cheaper
— but a larger shared context dilutes focus,
so it finds fewer defects.
The default stays fan-out:
recall comes first,
and `no` is an explicit, opt-in concession.

## Verification (step 6)

For each comment, isolate the key premise it rests on
— especially an external-system fact (Linux/POSIX behaviour, the System V ABI, Rust semantics).
Try to **refute** it:
re-read the cited code and consult an authoritative source.
Assign a verdict:

- **confirmed** — keep the comment unchanged.
- **uncertain** — keep it, but prefix `problem` with `(unverified) `.
- **refuted** — remove the comment,
  and append it to a `## Retracted by verification` list at the foot of the file with a one-line reason.

Remove **only** on confident refutation;
an unsure check is `uncertain`, not `refuted`.
This is the only step that may remove a comment, and only false positives.

## Consolidation (step 7)

Find clusters of comments that share one root cause or one fix
(e.g. several manual lock/unlock pairs that all want RAII).
For each cluster, write a single unified fix
and repoint each member's `**Fix.**` at it ("Shared with the other `raii` comments: …").
**Never remove a comment**
— every symptom stays at its own location.

## Output format

The assembled file is:
frontmatter (`date`, `mode`, `base` or `files`, `head`, `branch`, optional `title`),
a `# Summary`,
then a `## <section>` per persona in the order **Maintainability, Correctness, Security, Hardware, Documentation** (Correctness is the Development persona's section).
Each comment is a `### \`file\` line N` subsection:
a quoted ` ```diff ` snippet,
then a prose line `` `<grounding>` (<severity>): <problem> ``,
then a `**Fix.**` paragraph.
Do not restructure what the script emits;
only fill the summary and apply the verify/consolidate edits.

     8	    process::signal::{
     9	        constants::{SIGKILL, SIGSTOP},
    10	        sig_mask::SigMask,
    11	    },
    12	};
    13	
    14	pub fn sys_rt_sigprocmask(
    15	    how: u32,
    16	    set_ptr: Vaddr,
    17	    oldset_ptr: Vaddr,
    18	    sigset_size: usize,
    19	    ctx: &Context,
    20	) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    21	    let mask_op = MaskOp::try_from(how)?;
    22	    debug!(
    23	        "mask op = {:?}, set_ptr = 0x{:x}, oldset_ptr = 0x{:x}, sigset_size = {}",
    24	        mask_op, set_ptr, oldset_ptr, sigset_size
    25	    );
    26	    if sigset_size != 8 {
    27	        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "sigset size is not equal to 8");
    28	    }
    29	    do_rt_sigprocmask(mask_op, set_ptr, oldset_ptr, ctx)?;
    30	    Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(0))
    31	}
    32	
    33	fn do_rt_sigprocmask(
    34	    mask_op: MaskOp,
    35	    set_ptr: Vaddr,
    36	    oldset_ptr: Vaddr,
    37	    ctx: &Context,
    38	) -> Result<()> {
    39	    let old_sig_mask_value = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask().load(Ordering::Relaxed);
    40	    debug!("old sig mask value: 0x{:x}", old_sig_mask_value);
    41	    if oldset_ptr != 0 {
    42	        ctx.user_space()
    43	            .write_val(oldset_ptr, &old_sig_mask_value)?;
    44	    }
    45	
    46	    let sig_mask_ref = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask();
    47	    if set_ptr != 0 {
    48	        let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
    49	        match mask_op {
    50	            MaskOp::Block => {
    51	                // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
    52	                // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
    53	                read_mask -= SIGKILL;
    54	                read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
    55	                sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value + read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
    56	            }
    57	            MaskOp::Unblock => {
    58	                sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value - read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed)
    59	            }
    60	            MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
    61	        }
    62	    }
    63	    debug!("new set = {:x?}", sig_mask_ref.load(Ordering::Relaxed));
    64	
    65	    Ok(())
    66	}
    67	
    68	#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, TryFromInt)]
    69	#[repr(u32)]
    70	pub enum MaskOp {
    71	    Block = 0,
    72	    Unblock = 1,
    73	    SetMask = 2,
    74	}

     3	use core::sync::atomic::Ordering;
     4	
     5	use super::SyscallReturn;
     6	use crate::{
     7	    prelude::*,

     9	        constants::{SIGKILL, SIGSTOP},
    10	        sig_mask::SigMask,
    11	    },

    13	
    14	pub fn sys_rt_sigprocmask(
    15	    how: u32,
    16	    set_ptr: Vaddr,
    17	    oldset_ptr: Vaddr,
    18	    sigset_size: usize,
    19	    ctx: &Context,
    20	) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    21	    let mask_op = MaskOp::try_from(how)?;
    22	    debug!(
    23	        "mask op = {:?}, set_ptr = 0x{:x}, oldset_ptr = 0x{:x}, sigset_size = {}",
    24	        mask_op, set_ptr, oldset_ptr, sigset_size
    25	    );
    26	    if sigset_size != 8 {
    27	        error!("sigset size is not equal to 8");
    28	    }
    29	    do_rt_sigprocmask(mask_op, set_ptr, oldset_ptr, ctx)?;
    30	    Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(0))
    31	}
    32	
    33	fn do_rt_sigprocmask(
    34	    mask_op: MaskOp,
    35	    set_ptr: Vaddr,
    36	    oldset_ptr: Vaddr,
    37	    ctx: &Context,
    38	) -> Result<()> {
    39	    let old_sig_mask_value = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask().load(Ordering::Relaxed);
    40	    debug!("old sig mask value: 0x{:x}", old_sig_mask_value);
    41	    if oldset_ptr != 0 {
    42	        ctx.get_user_space()
    43	            .write_val(oldset_ptr, &old_sig_mask_value)?;
    44	    }
    45	
    46	    let sig_mask_ref = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask();
    47	    if set_ptr != 0 {
    48	        let mut read_mask = ctx.get_user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
    49	        match mask_op {
    50	            MaskOp::Block => {
    51	                // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
    52	                // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
    53	                read_mask -= SIGKILL;
    54	                read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
    55	                sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value + read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
    56	            }
    57	            MaskOp::Unblock => {
    58	                sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value - read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed)
    59	            }
    60	            MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
    61	        }
    62	    }
    63	    debug!("new set = {:x?}", sig_mask_ref.load(Ordering::Relaxed));
    64	
    65	    Ok(())
    66	}
    67	
    68	#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, TryFromInt)]
    69	#[repr(u32)]
    70	pub enum MaskOp {
    71	    Block = 0,
    72	    Unblock = 1,
    73	    SetMask = 2,
    74	}

   155	#[expect(clippy::too_many_arguments)]
   156	pub fn handle_user_signal(
   157	    ctx: &Context,
   158	    sig_num: SigNum,
   159	    handler_addr: Vaddr,
   160	    flags: SigActionFlags,
   161	    restorer_addr: Vaddr,
   162	    mut mask: SigMask,
   163	    user_ctx: &mut UserContext,
   164	    sig_info: siginfo_t,
   165	) -> Result<()> {
   166	    debug!("sig_num = {:?}, signame = {}", sig_num, sig_num.sig_name());
   167	    debug!("handler_addr = 0x{:x}", handler_addr);
   168	    debug!("flags = {:?}", flags);
   169	    debug!("restorer_addr = 0x{:x}", restorer_addr);
   170	
   171	    if flags.contains_unsupported_flag() {
   172	        warn!("Unsupported Signal flags: {:?}", flags);
   173	    }
   174	
   175	    if !flags.contains(SigActionFlags::SA_NODEFER) {
   176	        // Add current signal to mask
   177	        mask += sig_num;
   178	    }
   179	
   180	    // Block signals in sigmask when running signal handler
   181	    let old_mask = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask().load(Ordering::Relaxed);
   182	    ctx.posix_thread
   183	        .sig_mask()
   184	        .store(old_mask + mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
   185	
   186	    // Set up signal stack.
   187	    let mut stack_pointer = if let Some(sp) = use_alternate_signal_stack(ctx.thread_local) {
   188	        sp as u64
   189	    } else {
   190	        // Just use user stack
   191	        user_ctx.stack_pointer() as u64
   192	    };
   193	
   194	    // To avoid corrupting signal stack, we minus 128 first.
   195	    stack_pointer -= 128;
   196	
   197	    let user_space = ctx.user_space();
   198	
   199	    // 1. Write siginfo_t
   200	    stack_pointer -= mem::size_of::<siginfo_t>() as u64;
   201	    user_space.write_val(stack_pointer as _, &sig_info)?;
   202	    let siginfo_addr = stack_pointer;
   203	
   204	    // 2. Write ucontext_t.
   205

   207	        uc_sigmask: mask.into(),
   208	        ..Default::default()
   209	    };

   211	    let sig_context = ctx.thread_local.sig_context().get();
   212	    if let Some(sig_context_addr) = sig_context {
   213	        ucontext.uc_link = sig_context_addr;
   214	    } else {
   215	        ucontext.uc_link = 0;
   216	    }
   217

   220	    let fpu_context_bytes = fpu_context.as_bytes();
   221	    ctx.thread_local.fpu().set_context(FpuContext::new());
   222	
   223	    cfg_if::cfg_if! {
   224	        if #[cfg(target_arch = "x86_64")] {

   226	            // user program can use the XSAVE/XRSTOR instructions at that address,
   227	            // if necessary.
   228	            let fpu_context_addr =
   229	                alloc_aligned_in_user_stack(stack_pointer, fpu_context_bytes.len(), 64)?;

   232	                size_of::<ucontext_t>(),
   233	                align_of::<ucontext_t>(),
   234	            )?;
   235	            ucontext
   120	            // signal are generated while that signal is blocked, then only one
   121	            // instance of the signal is marked as pending (and the signal will be
   122	            // delivered just once when it is unblocked).  In the case where a
   123	            // standard signal is already pending, the siginfo_t structure (see
   124	            // sigaction(2)) associated with that signal is not overwritten on
   125	            // arrival of subsequent instances of the same signal.  Thus, the
   126	            // process will receive the information associated with the first
   127	            // instance of the signal.
   128	            let queue = self.get_std_queue_mut(signum);
   129	            if queue.is_some() {
   130	                // If there is already a signal pending, just ignore all subsequent signals
   131	                return false;
   132	            }
   133	            *queue = Some(signal);
   134	        } else {
   135	            // Real-time signals
   136	            let queue = self.get_rt_queue_mut(signum);
   137	            queue.push_back(signal);
   138	        }
   139	
   140	        true
   141	    }
   142	
   143	    fn dequeue(&mut self, blocked: &SigMask) -> Option<Box<dyn Signal>> {
   144	        // Deliver standard signals.
   145	        //
   146	        // According to signal(7):
   147	        // If both standard and real-time signals are pending for a process,
   148	        // POSIX leaves it unspecified which is delivered first. Linux, like
   149	        // many other implementations, gives priority to standard signals in
   150	        // this case.
   151	
   152	        // POSIX leaves unspecified which to deliver first if there are multiple
   153	        // pending standard signals. So we are free to define our own. The
   154	        // principle is to give more urgent signals higher priority (like SIGKILL).
   155	
   156	        // FIXME: the gvisor pty_test JobControlTest::ReleaseTTY requires that
   157	        // the SIGHUP signal should be handled before SIGCONT.
   158	        const ORDERED_STD_SIGS: [SigNum; COUNT_STD_SIGS] = [
   159	            SIGKILL, SIGTERM, SIGSTOP, SIGSEGV, SIGILL, SIGHUP, SIGCONT, SIGINT, SIGQUIT, SIGTRAP,
   160	            SIGABRT, SIGBUS, SIGFPE, SIGUSR1, SIGUSR2, SIGPIPE, SIGALRM, SIGSTKFLT, SIGCHLD,
   161	            SIGTSTP, SIGTTIN, SIGTTOU, SIGURG, SIGXCPU, SIGXFSZ, SIGVTALRM, SIGPROF, SIGWINCH,
   162	            SIGIO, SIGPWR, SIGSYS,
   163	        ];
   164	        for &signum in &ORDERED_STD_SIGS {
   165	            if blocked.contains(signum) {
   166	                continue;
   167	            }
   168	
   169	            let queue = self.get_std_queue_mut(signum);
   170	            let signal = queue.take();
   171	            if signal.is_some() {
   172	                return signal;
   173	            }
   174	        }
   175	
   176	        // If no standard signals, then deliver real-time signals.
   177	        //
   178	        // According to signal (7):
   179	        // Real-time signals are delivered in a guaranteed order.  Multiple
   180	        // real-time signals of the same type are delivered in the order
   181	        // they were sent.  If different real-time signals are sent to a
   182	        // process, they are delivered starting with the lowest-numbered
   183	        // signal.  (I.e., low-numbered signals have highest priority.)
   184	        for signum in MIN_RT_SIG_NUM..=MAX_RT_SIG_NUM {
   185	            let signum = SigNum::try_from(signum).unwrap();
   186	            if blocked.contains(signum) {
   187	                continue;
   188	            }
   189	
   190	            let queue = self.get_rt_queue_mut(signum);
   191	            let signal = queue.pop_front();
   192	            if signal.is_some() {
   193	                return signal;
   194	            }
   195	        }
   196	
   197	        // There must be pending but blocked signals
   198	        None
   199	    }
   200	
   201	    /// Returns whether the `SigQueues` has some pending signals which are not blocked
   202	    fn has_pending(&self, blocked: SigMask) -> bool {
   203	        self.std_queues.iter().any(|signal| {
   204	            signal
   205	                .as_ref()

 succeeded in 0ms:
   180	}
   181	
   182	impl Pause for WaitQueue {
   183	    fn pause_until_or_timeout_impl<F, R>(
   184	        &self,
   185	        mut cond: F,
   186	        timeout: Option<&ManagedTimeout>,
   187	    ) -> Result<R>
   188	    where
   189	        F: FnMut() -> Option<R>,
   190	    {
   191	        // Fast path:
   192	        if let Some(res) = cond() {
   193	            return Ok(res);
   194	        }
   195	
   196	        let (waiter, _) = Waiter::new_pair();
   197	        let cond = || {
   198	            self.enqueue(waiter.waker());
   199	            cond()
   200	        };
   201	        waiter.pause_until_or_timeout_impl(cond, timeout)
   202	    }
   203	
   204	    fn pause_timeout(&self, _timeout: &TimeoutExt<'_>) -> Result<()> {
   205	        panic!("`pause_timeout` can only be used on `Waiter`");
   206	    }
   207	}
   208	
   209	/// Executes a closure after temporarily adjusting the signal mask of the current POSIX thread.
   210	pub fn with_sigmask_changed<R>(
   211	    ctx: &Context,
   212	    mask_op: impl FnOnce(SigMask) -> SigMask,
   213	    operate: impl FnOnce() -> R,
   214	) -> R {
   215	    let sig_mask = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask();
   216	
   217	    // Save the original signal mask and apply the mask updates.
   218	    let old_mask = sig_mask.load(Ordering::Relaxed);
   219	    sig_mask.store(mask_op(old_mask), Ordering::Relaxed);
   220	
   221	    // Perform the operation.
   222	    let res = operate();
   223	
   224	    // Restore the original signal mask.
   225	    sig_mask.store(old_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
   226	
   227	    res
   228	}

   231	mod test {
   232	    use core::sync::atomic::AtomicBool;
   233	
   234	    use ostd::prelude::*;
   235	
   236	    use super::*;
   237	    use crate::thread::{kernel_thread::ThreadOptions, Thread};
   238	
   239	    #[ktest]
   240	    fn test_waiter_pause() {
   241	        let wait_queue = Arc::new(WaitQueue::new());
   242	        let wait_queue_cloned = wait_queue.clone();
   243	
   244	        let boolean = Arc::new(AtomicBool::new(false));
   245	        let boolean_cloned = boolean.clone();
   246	
   247	        let thread = ThreadOptions::new(move || {
   248	            Thread::yield_now();
   249	
   250	            boolean_cloned.store(true, Ordering::Relaxed);
   251	            wait_queue_cloned.wake_all();
   252	        })
   253	        .spawn();
   254	
   255	        wait_queue
    45	        thread_local.sig_context().set(Some(ucontext.uc_link));
    46	    };
    47	    ucontext.uc_mcontext.copy_user_regs_to(user_ctx);
    48	
    49	    // Restore FPU context on stack
    50	    cfg_if::cfg_if! {
    51	        if #[cfg(target_arch = "x86_64")] {
    52	            let fpu_context_addr = ucontext.uc_mcontext.fpu_context_addr();
    53	        } else if #[cfg(any(target_arch = "riscv64", target_arch = "loongarch64"))] {
    54	            // In RISC-V/LoongArch64, FPU context is placed directly after `ucontext_t` on signal stack.
    55	            let fpu_context_addr = sig_context_addr + size_of::<ucontext_t>();
    56	        } else {
    57	            compile_error!("unsupported target");
    58	        }
    59	    }
    60	    let mut fpu_context = FpuContext::new();
    61	    let mut fpu_context_writer = VmWriter::from(fpu_context.as_bytes_mut());
    62	    ctx.user_space()
    63	        .read_bytes(fpu_context_addr, &mut fpu_context_writer)?;
    64	    ctx.thread_local.fpu().set_context(fpu_context);
    65	
    66	    // unblock sig mask
    67	    let sig_mask = ucontext.uc_sigmask;
    68	    let old_mask = posix_thread.sig_mask().load(Ordering::Relaxed);
    69	    posix_thread
    70	        .sig_mask()
    71	        .store(old_mask - sig_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
    72	
    73	    Ok(SyscallReturn::NoReturn)
    74	}

commit 8a801676ab2f827e1d6e4d1ec6803261b5e7859c
Author:     Wei Zhang <ruoyuan.zw@antgroup.com>
AuthorDate: Thu Jul 17 08:55:05 2025 +0000
Commit:     Jianfeng Jiang <jiangjianfeng.jjf@antgroup.com>
CommitDate: Mon Jul 28 17:08:10 2025 +0800

    Disallow masking SIGKILL and SIGSTOP in user space
    
    According to the Linux man pages[1], "It is not possible to block SIGKILL
    or SIGSTOP. Attempts to do so are silently ignored." This patch ensures
    compliance by explicitly removing SIGKILL and SIGSTOP from the signal
    mask in the `MaskOp::SetMask` operation of the `rt_sigprocmask` syscall.
    
    [1]: https://www.man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/sigprocmask.2.html

+++ b/kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs
@@ -57,7 +57,11 @@ fn do_rt_sigprocmask(
             MaskOp::Unblock => {
                 sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value - read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed)
             }
-            MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
+            MaskOp::SetMask => {
+                read_mask -= SIGKILL; // Cannot block SIGKILL
+                read_mask -= SIGSTOP; // Cannot block SIGSTOP
+                sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
+            }
         }
     }
     debug!("new set = {:x?}", sig_mask_ref.load(Ordering::Relaxed));

    16	
    17	pub fn sys_rt_sigprocmask(
    18	    how: u32,
    19	    set_ptr: Vaddr,
    20	    oldset_ptr: Vaddr,
    21	    sigset_size: usize,
    22	    ctx: &Context,
    23	) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    24	    let mask_op = MaskOp::try_from(how)?;
    25	    debug!(
    26	        "mask op = {:?}, set_ptr = 0x{:x}, oldset_ptr = 0x{:x}, sigset_size = {}",
    27	        mask_op, set_ptr, oldset_ptr, sigset_size
    28	    );
    29	    if sigset_size != 8 {
    30	        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "sigset size is not equal to 8");
    31	    }
    32	    do_rt_sigprocmask(mask_op, set_ptr, oldset_ptr, ctx)?;
    33	    Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(0))
    34	}
    35	
    36	fn do_rt_sigprocmask(
    37	    mask_op: MaskOp,
    38	    set_ptr: Vaddr,
    39	    oldset_ptr: Vaddr,
    40	    ctx: &Context,
    41	) -> Result<()> {
    42	    let old_sig_mask_value = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask();
    43	    debug!("old sig mask value: 0x{:x}", old_sig_mask_value);
    44	    if oldset_ptr != 0 {
    45	        ctx.user_space()
    46	            .write_val(oldset_ptr, &old_sig_mask_value)?;
    47	    }
    48	
    49	    if set_ptr != 0 {
    50	        let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
    51	        match mask_op {
    52	            MaskOp::Block => {
    53	                // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
    54	                // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
    55	                read_mask -= SIGKILL;
    56	                read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
    57	                ctx.set_sig_mask(old_sig_mask_value + read_mask);
    58	            }
    59	            MaskOp::Unblock => {
    60	                ctx.set_sig_mask(old_sig_mask_value - read_mask);
    61	            }
    62	            MaskOp::SetMask => {
    63	                read_mask -= SIGKILL; // Cannot block SIGKILL
    64	                read_mask -= SIGSTOP; // Cannot block SIGSTOP
    65	                ctx.set_sig_mask(read_mask);
    66	            }
    67	        }
    68	    }
    69	    debug!("new set = {:x?}", ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask());
    70	
    71	    Ok(())
    72	}
    73	
    74	#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, TryFromInt)]
    75	#[repr(u32)]
    76	pub enum MaskOp {
    77	    Block = 0,
    78	    Unblock = 1,
    79	    SetMask = 2,
    80	}

# Summary

<!-- SUMMARY -->

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 9

> ```diff
>         constants::{SIGKILL, SIGSTOP},
>         sig_mask::SigMask,
> ```

`qualified-fn-imports` (nit): The direct imports of `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from `process::signal::constants` obscure their origin at each use site.

**Fix.** Import `constants` and use `constants::SIGKILL` and `constants::SIGSTOP`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 26

> ```diff
>     if sigset_size != 8 {
>         return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "sigset size is not equal to 8");
>     }
> ```

`no-magic-number` (minor): The literal `8` encodes the Linux kernel signal-set size in bytes, but its ABI meaning and source are not expressed at this check.

**Fix.** Define a shared constant such as `KERNEL_SIGSET_SIZE_BYTES`, document its source, and use it for signal-set size checks.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 51

> ```diff
>                 // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
>                 // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> ```

`cite-sources` (minor): The attribution `According to man pages` is too vague to verify the external `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` masking rule.

**Fix.** Cite the precise `sigprocmask(2)` section here, or on the shared helper that implements this rule.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 53

> ```diff
>                 read_mask -= SIGKILL;
>                 read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> ```

`dry` (minor): The `SIGKILL`/`SIGSTOP` filtering policy is also implemented manually in `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigsuspend.rs`, `kernel/src/syscall/signalfd.rs`, and `kernel/src/process/signal/sig_action.rs`, leaving the same invariant represented in four places.

**Fix.** Add a single `SigMask` operation such as `without_unblockable_signals()` and use it at all mask-construction boundaries.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 68

> ```diff
> #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, TryFromInt)]
> #[repr(u32)]
> pub enum MaskOp {
> ```

`alphabetical-attrs` (nit): `MaskOp` places `#[derive(...)]` before `#[repr(u32)]`, and the derived traits are not alphabetically ordered.

**Fix.** Order the attributes as `#[repr(u32)]` followed by `#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, Eq, PartialEq, TryFromInt)]`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 70

> ```diff
> pub enum MaskOp {
>     Block = 0,
>     Unblock = 1,
>     SetMask = 2,
> }
> ```

`narrow-visibility` (minor): `MaskOp` has no consumers outside this private `rt_sigprocmask` module, so `pub` unnecessarily exposes an implementation detail.

**Fix.** Remove `pub` from `MaskOp` until an external consumer requires wider visibility.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 21

> ```diff
>     let mask_op = MaskOp::try_from(how)?;
> ```

Incorrect argument validation (minor): `how` is validated even when `set_ptr == 0`, although `rt_sigprocmask` must ignore `how` when no new mask is supplied. For example, a request with an unsupported `how`, `set_ptr == 0`, and a valid `oldset_ptr` should return the current mask, but this returns `EINVAL`.

**Fix.** Defer `MaskOp::try_from(how)` until the `set_ptr != 0` branch; pass the raw `how` into `do_rt_sigprocmask` or otherwise avoid converting it for mask-query-only calls.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 41

> ```diff
>     if oldset_ptr != 0 {
>         ctx.user_space()
>             .write_val(oldset_ptr, &old_sig_mask_value)?;
>     }
> 
>     let sig_mask_ref = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask();
>     if set_ptr != 0 {
>         let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
> ```

Incorrect error side effects (minor): The syscall writes `oldset_ptr` before reading and applying `set_ptr`, reversing Linux's observable operation order. With an invalid `set_ptr` and valid `oldset_ptr`, this modifies `oldset` before returning `EFAULT`; with a valid `set_ptr` and invalid `oldset_ptr`, it returns `EFAULT` without applying the requested mask. Linux reads the new set and updates the mask before copying the old set out.

**Fix.** Read and validate `set_ptr` first, apply the requested mask while retaining the previous value locally, and only then write that previous value to `oldset_ptr`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 60

> ```diff
>             MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
> ```

Incorrect signal masking (major): `MaskOp::SetMask` stores `read_mask` without removing `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP`. A caller can include either signal in a `SIG_SETMASK` request, after which signal dequeue treats it as blocked and the process can ignore even `SIGKILL`.

**Fix.** Remove `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from `read_mask` before storing it for `MaskOp::SetMask`, as already done for `MaskOp::Block`.

## Security

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 60

> ```diff
> @@
> -            MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
> +            MaskOp::SetMask => {
> +                read_mask -= SIGKILL;
> +                read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> +                sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
> +            }
> ```

Failure to ignore unmaskable signals (critical): `MaskOp::SetMask` stores the user-provided `read_mask` without removing `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP`. A process can call `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, &mask_with_SIGKILL, NULL, 8)`, causing the signal queue to treat `SIGKILL` as blocked; a later `kill -9` can remain pending instead of terminating the process.

**Fix.** Filter the unmaskable signals before every operation that installs a mask, including `MaskOp::SetMask`:

```rust
read_mask -= SIGKILL;
read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
match mask_op {
    MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
    // ...
}
```

# Summary

The file is tight and the syscall logic stays in one place, which makes the signal-mask flow easy to follow. The biggest issues are semantic: `SIG_SETMASK` still accepts `SIGKILL`/`SIGSTOP`, `how` is rejected even when only reading the old mask, and the old mask is copied out before the new mask is validated and applied. Smaller follow-ups are mostly style and maintenance around visibility, the hard-coded sigset size, and duplicated mask normalization.

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 26

> ```diff
>     if sigset_size != 8 {
>         return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "sigset size is not equal to 8");
>     }
> ```

`no-magic-number` (minor): The literal `8` encodes the Linux kernel signal-set size in bytes, but its ABI meaning and source are not expressed at this check.

**Fix.** Define a shared constant such as `KERNEL_SIGSET_SIZE_BYTES`, document its source, and use it for signal-set size checks.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 51

> ```diff
>                 // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
>                 // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> ```

`cite-sources` (minor): The attribution `According to man pages` is too vague to verify the external `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` masking rule.

**Fix.** Cite the precise `sigprocmask(2)` section here, or on the shared helper that implements this rule.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 53

> ```diff
>                 read_mask -= SIGKILL;
>                 read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> ```

`dry` (minor): The `SIGKILL`/`SIGSTOP` filtering policy is also implemented manually in `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigsuspend.rs`, `kernel/src/syscall/signalfd.rs`, and `kernel/src/process/signal/sig_action.rs`, leaving the same invariant represented in four places.

**Fix.** Add a single `SigMask` operation such as `without_unblockable_signals()` and use it at all mask-construction boundaries.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 68

> ```diff
> #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, TryFromInt)]
> #[repr(u32)]
> pub enum MaskOp {
> ```

`alphabetical-attrs` (nit): `MaskOp` places `#[derive(...)]` before `#[repr(u32)]`, and the derived traits are not alphabetically ordered.

**Fix.** Order the attributes as `#[repr(u32)]` followed by `#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, Eq, PartialEq, TryFromInt)]`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 70

> ```diff
> pub enum MaskOp {
>     Block = 0,
>     Unblock = 1,
>     SetMask = 2,
> }
> ```

`narrow-visibility` (minor): `MaskOp` has no consumers outside this private `rt_sigprocmask` module, so `pub` unnecessarily exposes an implementation detail.

**Fix.** Remove `pub` from `MaskOp` until an external consumer requires wider visibility.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 21

> ```diff
>     let mask_op = MaskOp::try_from(how)?;
> ```

Incorrect argument validation (minor): `how` is validated even when `set_ptr == 0`, although `rt_sigprocmask` must ignore `how` when no new mask is supplied. For example, a request with an unsupported `how`, `set_ptr == 0`, and a valid `oldset_ptr` should return the current mask, but this returns `EINVAL`.

**Fix.** Defer `MaskOp::try_from(how)` until the `set_ptr != 0` branch; pass the raw `how` into `do_rt_sigprocmask` or otherwise avoid converting it for mask-query-only calls.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 41

> ```diff
>     if oldset_ptr != 0 {
>         ctx.user_space()
>             .write_val(oldset_ptr, &old_sig_mask_value)?;
>     }
> 
>     let sig_mask_ref = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask();
>     if set_ptr != 0 {
>         let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
> ```

Incorrect error side effects (minor): The syscall writes `oldset_ptr` before reading and applying `set_ptr`, reversing Linux's observable operation order. With an invalid `set_ptr` and valid `oldset_ptr`, this modifies `oldset` before returning `EFAULT`; with a valid `set_ptr` and invalid `oldset_ptr`, it returns `EFAULT` without applying the requested mask. Linux reads the new set and updates the mask before copying the old set out.

**Fix.** Read and validate `set_ptr` first, apply the requested mask while retaining the previous value locally, and only then write that previous value to `oldset_ptr`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 60

> ```diff
>             MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
> ```

Incorrect signal masking (major): `MaskOp::SetMask` stores `read_mask` without removing `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP`. A caller can include either signal in a `SIG_SETMASK` request, after which signal dequeue treats it as blocked and the process can ignore even `SIGKILL`.

**Fix.** Remove `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from `read_mask` before storing it for `MaskOp::SetMask`, as already done for `MaskOp::Block`.

## Security

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 60

> ```diff
> @@
> -            MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
> +            MaskOp::SetMask => {
> +                read_mask -= SIGKILL;
> +                read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> +                sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
> +            }
> ```

Failure to ignore unmaskable signals (critical): `MaskOp::SetMask` stores the user-provided `read_mask` without removing `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP`. A process can call `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, &mask_with_SIGKILL, NULL, 8)`, causing the signal queue to treat `SIGKILL` as blocked; a later `kill -9` can remain pending instead of terminating the process.

**Fix.** Filter the unmaskable signals before every operation that installs a mask, including `MaskOp::SetMask`:

```rust

read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
match mask_op {
    MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
    // ...
}
```

===== EXPECTED DEFECTS =====
# Expected defects

1. location: kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs (persona: development, grounding: Incorrect signal semantics, severity: critical)
   defect: `sys_rt_sigprocmask` sanitizes `read_mask` for `MaskOp::Block`, but the `MaskOp::SetMask` arm stores the user-provided mask verbatim with `sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed)`. A caller using `SIG_SETMASK` can therefore place `SIGKILL` or `SIGSTOP` in the thread's blocked signal mask. Linux requires attempts to block these two signals to be silently ignored, so accepting them in the set-mask path can make user tasks unkillable or unstoppable and breaks signal semantics.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag that every path installing a blocked signal mask, including `SIG_SETMASK`, must preserve the invariant that `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` cannot be blocked.

2. location: kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs (persona: development, grounding: Incorrect argument validation, severity: major)
   defect: `sys_rt_sigprocmask` converts `how` with `MaskOp::try_from(how)?` before it checks whether `set_ptr` is null. Linux ignores the `how` argument when `set` is `NULL`, because that call only reads the current signal mask through `oldset`. A call such as `rt_sigprocmask(999, NULL, oldset, 8)` should therefore succeed and report the old mask, but this implementation returns `EINVAL` before it reaches the `oldset_ptr` write.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag that `rt_sigprocmask` must ignore invalid `how` values when `set` is `NULL`; only calls that install or change a signal mask should validate the operation.

3. location: kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs (persona: maintainability, grounding: narrow-visibility, severity: minor)
   defect: `MaskOp` is declared `pub`, but it is only used inside `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` to decode the syscall's `how` argument. Exposing this syscall-local enum creates an unnecessary module interface for an implementation detail that no other module needs to name.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag that syscall-local decode enums should not be public when all uses are contained in the same source file.

>     let sig_mask_ref = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask();
>     if set_ptr != 0 {
>         let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
> ```

Incorrect error side effects (minor): The syscall writes `oldset_ptr` before reading and applying `set_ptr`, reversing Linux's observable operation order. With an invalid `set_ptr` and valid `oldset_ptr`, this modifies `oldset` before returning `EFAULT`; with a valid `set_ptr` and invalid `oldset_ptr`, it returns `EFAULT` without applying the requested mask. Linux reads the new set and updates the mask before copying the old set out.

**Fix.** Read and validate `set_ptr` first, apply the requested mask while retaining the previous value locally, and only then write that previous value to `oldset_ptr`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 60

> ```diff
>             MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
> ```

Incorrect signal masking (major): `MaskOp::SetMask` stores `read_mask` without removing `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP`. A caller can include either signal in a `SIG_SETMASK` request, after which signal dequeue treats it as blocked and the process can ignore even `SIGKILL`.

**Fix.** Remove `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from `read_mask` before storing it for `MaskOp::SetMask`, as already done for `MaskOp::Block`.

## Security

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 60

> ```diff
> @@
> -            MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
> +            MaskOp::SetMask => {
> +                read_mask -= SIGKILL;
> +                read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> +                sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
> +            }
> ```

Failure to ignore unmaskable signals (critical): `MaskOp::SetMask` stores the user-provided `read_mask` without removing `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP`. A process can call `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, {prompt}mask_with_SIGKILL, NULL, 8)`, causing the signal queue to treat `SIGKILL` as blocked; a later `kill -9` can remain pending instead of terminating the process.

**Fix.** Filter the unmaskable signals before every operation that installs a mask, including `MaskOp::SetMask`:

```rust
read_mask -= SIGKILL;
read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
match mask_op {
    MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
    // ...
}
```
